One new architectural delta I found
This is important.
The August 23 G/G+ lifelong-memory clarification is newer than GitHub PR #89. So GitHub currently contains the strong
Stage G qualification architecture, but it does not yet canonically ratify all of the newly clarified G/G+ boundary.
The new handoff establishes:

  Stage G validates the substrate and measures cognitive retrieval weaknesses. G+ builds the cognitive retrieval machinery.
   ALICE_STAGE_G_GPLUS_MEMORY_ARCHITECTURE_HANDOFF_2026-08-23(2).md


And Stage G must now include a long-horizon cognitive recall baseline covering:

1. explicit recall;
2. indirect recall;
3. spontaneous-recall candidate discovery.

For spontaneous recall, Stage G does not need the full cognitive controller yet. It needs to measure whether the relevant old
memory is retrievable at all, which path found it, its candidate rank, whether it entered context, latency/cost, and false
candidates. ALICE_STAGE_G_GPLUS_MEMORY_ARCHITECTURE_HANDOFF_2026-08-23(2).md
Then G+ builds the actual Memory Attention / Retrieval Controller.
Phase 2 remains canonical through both G and G+. Passing either one does not grant canonical authority. The existing H → I → J
→ owner-acceptance chain still governs final replacement. ALICE_STAGE_G_GPLUS_MEMORY_ARCHITECTURE_HANDOFF_2026-08-23(2).md
ALICE_STAGE_G_GPLUS_MEMORY_ARCHITECTURE_HANDOFF_2026-08-23(2).md

That is the main new architectural change we need to install into the repo.

I would not touch GitHub main or alter the tournament's scientific inputs while this tournament is running. We
currently have an exceptionally clean reproducibility anchor: tournament + local repo + remote GitHub all point to 0abaed... .
There is no benefit in disturbing that during qualification.
Our sequence from here should therefore be:

The good news is substantial. All four public qualification batches finished, all 12/12 generators are viable, and all 9 judge
families are viable. The controller therefore correctly set public_runtime_qualification_passed=true and

public qualification stage have all passed.
The failure happened immediately afterward at:
STEP 2: CANDIDATE-ONLY LEAKAGE-SAFE MC8 MATERIALIZATION

Specifically, scan_gold() could not reconstruct 290 router source-span IDs, beginning with elaina-canon-0001.u01 , .u02 ,
.u03 , etc. Pasted text(20260824-042829).txt

That pattern is important. This does not look like random missing data or a Kaggle failure. The missing IDs have a systematic
 <record>.uNN shape. My leading hypothesis is that qual_core_v1.py is reconstructing the router against the current owner-
ratified gold using an incorrect schema/ID assumption-for example, expecting the unit IDs in one field/layout while the G2A
gold actually stores the semantic units another way. We already had an architectural lesson earlier in this exact tournament work:
do not infer JSON schemas; read the actual upstream bytes. ChatGPT-Recovery Audit Progress-20260823-2255.pdf
Most importantly, the firewall did its job. At the moment of failure:

  $W = "C:\ALICE_Vault\datasets\memory_stage_g2\alice.stage-g2.g2a.gold-semantic-decomposition.v1"

  $Out = "$HOME\Downloads\ALICE_MC8_Materialization_Debug_Files.zip"
  $Tmp = Join-Path $env:TEMP "alice-mc8-debug-files"

  $Files = @(
      "$W\audits\eipm-global-fidelity-router-owner-ratified-v2\eipm_global_fidelity_router_v2_entries.jsonl",

      "$W\gold\priority2-owner-ratified-gold-v1\g2a_priority2_ratified_unit_index.jsonl",

      "$W\gold\priority3-owner-ratified-gold-v1\g2a_priority3_ratified_unit_index.jsonl",

      "$W\gold\priority4a-owner-ratified-gold-v1\g2a_priority4a_ratified_unit_index.jsonl",

      "$W\gold\priority1-owner-ratified-gold-v1\g2a_priority1_owner_ratified_gold_lock_manifest.json",

      "$W\gold\priority1-owner-ratified-gold-v1\g2a_priority1_owner_corrections_and_supplements.json",

      "$W\passes\gold-candidate-pass2-dual-axis-review-v1\g2a_gold_candidate_pass2.jsonl"
  )

  Write-Host "`n===== VERIFYING FILES =====" -ForegroundColor Cyan

  foreach ($F in $Files) {
      if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $F -PathType Leaf)) {
          throw "MISSING FILE: $F"
      }

      $Hash = (Get-FileHash -LiteralPath $F -Algorithm SHA256).Hash
      Write-Host "OK $F"
      Write-Host "    SHA256=$Hash"
  }

  Write-Host "`n===== BUILDING ZIP =====" -ForegroundColor Cyan

  if (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Tmp) {
      Remove-Item -LiteralPath $Tmp -Recurse -Force
  }

  New-Item -ItemType Directory -Path $Tmp | Out-Null

  foreach ($F in $Files) {
      Copy-Item -LiteralPath $F -Destination $Tmp
  }

  if (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Out) {
      Remove-Item -LiteralPath $Out -Force
  }

  Compress-Archive `
      -Path "$Tmp\*" `
      -DestinationPath $Out `

 Remove-Item -LiteralPath $Tmp -Recurse -Force

 Write-Host "SHA256=$((Get-FileHash -LiteralPath $Out -Algorithm SHA256).Hash)"
 Write-Host "SIZE=$((Get-Item -LiteralPath $Out).Length) bytes"

 Write-Host "`nZIP CONTENTS:"
 Add-Type -AssemblyName System.IO.Compression.FileSystem
 $Z = [System.IO.Compression.ZipFile]::OpenRead($Out)
 $Z.Entries | ForEach-Object { Write-Host " - $($_.FullName)" }

The reason this can take a long time is important. Batch 1 contains 3 models:


                                          🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                                   18 / 254
  qwen3.8:27b
  qwen3.6:27b
  qwen3.6:35b-a3b


And the worker processes those models sequentially inside one Kaggle kernel. For each model it does roughly:

  fresh model pull
  → start Ollama
  → public smoke verification
  → generate all 32 Round-1 tasks
  → validate/store output
  → unload/delete model
  → next model


So this first kernel represents roughly 3 large model downloads + 96 generation tasks, not one inference.
Also, each generation task is allowed up to 4096 output tokens, with up to 3 attempts, and an individual inference timeout of
1800 seconds (30 min). That means even a single pathological generation can consume significant time before the worker
gives up/retries.
So ~2 hours is long, but not evidence that it is hung yet. The repeated state is genuinely KernelWorkerStatus.RUNNING , not
ERROR , FAILED , or a local polling loop pretending to work. Pasted text(20260824-063535).txt

tracker-status.jsonl gives us timestamped machine-readable history, so later we can calculate exactly how long each
batch/model group took instead of estimating.
One limitation is deliberate: Kaggle doesn't reliably expose live stdout from a running private kernel. So for a 3-model sequential
batch, the tracker knows the exact model queue but won't falsely claim whether it's currently on model 1, 2, or 3. Once the
output becomes available, we can determine the actual per-model elapsed times.

Why the old tournament is the wrong shape
The original plan is:

  Round 1: 12 models × 32 tasks
  Round 2: 5 models × 64 new tasks
  Final:    3 models × 269 new tasks


That's:

  384
  + 320
  + 807
  ─────
  1,511 generator outputs


And each candidate needs four independent non-generator-family judge evaluations under the current tournament rules, so the
full design implies roughly:

  1,511 × 4
  = 6,044 candidate/judge evaluations


We built this as if the tournament itself had to exhaustively validate the entire MC8 distribution.
It doesn't.
The tournament's only job is:

  pick the best generator reliably enough to proceed with MC10B.

The actual MC10B pipeline is where the winner gets exercised across the real workload, provenance gates, falsification, E-INF
boundaries, etc.
So spending hundreds of generations on models we're going to discard is the wrong allocation.
The timing data proves it. Batch 1 completed and Batch 2 started around 07:30 UTC; Batch 2 completed before Batch 3 started
around 09:43 UTC. Pasted text(20260824-121222).txt Batch 3 was much faster and Batch 4 then began around 10:17 UTC.
Pasted text(20260824-121222).txt The huge model-to-model runtime variance means fixed equal budgets are especially
wasteful.
Kaggle's current docs also still describe T4×2 notebook sessions with a 12-hour execution ceiling, and GPU availability may
involve queues. So I don't want the redesign's correctness or usability to depend on us magically obtaining lots of parallel GPU
kernels. (Kaggle)

I would change it to this.

Use 16 carefully stratified tasks instead of 32.
The current selector already has 8 structural strata. So we take:

  2 examples × 8 strata = 16


This remains deterministic and balanced.
Crucially, the new 16-task set can be selected as a subset of the original Round-1 32.
Therefore:

  Batch 1 models: reuse existing outputs
  Batch 2 models: reuse existing outputs
  Batch 3 models: reuse existing outputs


That's 9 models × 16 tasks requiring zero new generation.
Only these three Batch-4 models need to run:

  nemotron-cascade-2:30b-a3b
  nemotron-3.5-lightning:30b-a3b
  olmo-3.1:32b


So new screening generation becomes:

  3 × 16 = 48


instead of redoing:

  12 × 32 = 384


Huge difference.
We derive the 16-task subset from the accepted 32-task outputs. We do not alter the original outputs; v2 gets a new workroot
and records exactly which v1.1.6 records were imported.


Stage C - rank 12 → top 5
Judge those 16 common tasks.
Keep the existing important invariants:

  candidate IDs blinded
  same-family judge blocked
  evaluator gold unavailable during generation
  candidate freeze before evaluator gold opens
  4 independent judge families/candidate
  judge consensus != truth evidence
  zero-gate failures remain hard failures


We keep the existing fidelity score function too.
So we're not weakening what "good" means.
We're reducing how many examples we need before eliminating obviously inferior models.

Advance:

  12 → 5


with the existing family-diversity protection.


Stage D - top 5 get only 8 new tasks
Not 64.

  Round 1: 16
  Round 2: 24


Generation:

  5 × 8 = 40


The new 8 aren't random. They should be chosen from strata where Round-1 produced:

  high judge disagreement
  close model scores
  high invalidity
  high hidden-recovery variance

This is the important redesign.
The benchmark becomes adaptive.
We spend new evidence specifically where it can change the ranking.

  5 → 3

Cumulative:

  16 + 8 + 16 = 40 tasks


Generation:

  3 × 16 = 48


These should emphasize:

    hardest pseudo-gaps
    relationship-sensitive cases
    contradiction preservation
    source-person distinctiveness
    cases where generic flattening is tempting
    evidence-conditioned UNKNOWN vs invention
    cross-context/temporal reconstruction

Then full four-family independent judging.

Stage F - confidence gate
This is the other major change.
Don't automatically continue merely because another round exists.
If #1 clearly beats #2:

  STOP
  winner selected


If they're statistically/behaviorally too close:

  Top 2 only
  +16 targeted tie-break tasks


Then stop.
So the expensive tail occurs only if it can actually change our decision.

One more architecture change: one generator per Kaggle job

  one Kaggle kernel

  Kernel B → one model
  Kernel C → one model

Because Batch 3 showed that some models are dramatically faster than others. With 3-model kernels, one slow model holds the
whole batch hostage.
Single-model jobs give us:

  fine-grained resume
  per-model runtime
  clean failure isolation
  easy cancellation

  no slow-model head-of-line blocking

Fidelity remains more important than speed

we are not turning this into a speed competition.
Ranking priority remains:

  1. fidelity / reconstruction quality
  2. provenance + hard-gate validity
  3. distinctiveness / non-genericness
  4. uncertainty calibration
  5. consistency


Runtime is only:

  an operational tie-breaker between near-equivalent generators


A slower model that meaningfully reconstructs Elaina better still wins.

I’m not going to assume the redesign is right. Given what the winner will be used for, I’m treating this as a model-selection
problem under high downstream risk, not a runtime optimization problem. I’m comparing exhaustive evaluation, successive-
halving/racing, judge-ensemble design, hidden holdouts, and statistical confidence methods before deciding whether we should
stop the current run.
One important finding is changing my answer: for Alice, judge-score ranking alone is not enough, even if we make it
statistically efficient. Research on synthetic-data generators finds that “best at answering” and “best at generating training data”
can diverge, and generator quality is better established by the downstream effect of the produced data. I’m incorporating a small
downstream utility gate for the finalists rather than selecting purely from MC8-style judgments.
I’m now mapping the research back onto the actual v1.1.6 code rather than designing in the abstract—specifically the 32/64/269
split, stratum selector, hard gates, judge-family exclusion, and scoring. That matters because the right answer may be “keep
more of Round 1 than I first proposed,” not simply shrink everything.

I did the research and audited the actual v1.1.6 tournament logic. The conclusion is not “yes, the redesign I gave you before was
perfect.”
The earlier 16 → +8 → +16 redesign was too aggressive for something this important.
What I would use for Alice is a hybrid adaptive tournament with a real final holdout and downstream canary. And
because of that conclusion, do not stop the currently running Batch 4 yet. Let it finish the 32-task screen for the last three
models.

Why I changed the design
There is strong evidence that exhaustive LLM evaluation is often unnecessary. ICLR 2025 work on adaptive evaluation found that
sequentially choosing informative model/sample evaluations could identify the best method using roughly 5-15% of the normal
resources. ICML 2025 similarly showed that adaptive testing can make model evaluation substantially more efficient, and ACL
2025 work reported large benchmark reductions while preserving rankings. (ICLR Proceedings)
But that doesn't mean “just use 16 samples.” Active-testing research specifically warns that adaptively choosing test items
introduces selection bias unless the estimator/design accounts for it. And the very recent literature on ranking uncertainty shows
that apparent leaderboard ordering can vary substantially across benchmark subsets. (Proceedings of Machine Learning
Research)
For Alice, that second point matters more than raw cost savings. We don't have thousands of previous generators and a validated
benchmark-compression model telling us that 16 particular MC8 items preserve the full ranking. Throwing away good models
after only 16 examples would be unjustifiably risky.
And the current Round 1 is actually good: v1.1.6 selected 32 tasks as 4 tasks × 8 distinct strata. So I would preserve that
entire first stage. Your run has already completed those 32 for nine generators; Batch 4 is producing them for the remaining
three. The successful public qualification and leakage-safe 365-task materialization are also already established. Pasted
text(20260824-121222).txt

There are bigger problems with the old tournament than the number of tasks
I inspected the actual v1.1.6 code. Four things concern me more than the 365-task final.
First, advancement is currently essentially a fixed 12 → 5 → 3 ranking. There are no confidence intervals around those ranks. A
model can therefore be fifth rather than sixth because of sampling/judge noise, and we simply eliminate #6.
Second, the current advancement logic deliberately forces candidate-family diversity into the top five and top three. That's useful
for exploration, but it is not appropriate as a hard rule for choosing Alice's best generator. If the three best models are
genuinely Qwen-family models, we should not promote a worse model merely because it has another family name. Family
diversity is critical for the judges, not necessarily the candidates.
Third, a judge can see several blinded candidates for the same task in one evaluation prompt. Research now shows that LLM
judging has meaningful position/presentation effects, and pairwise/comparative evaluation can amplify superficial preferences.
LLM judges can also be non-transitive: A beats B, B beats C, while C beats A. (ACL Anthology)
Fourth, simply adding more judgments does not magically solve judge bias. Independent judge ensembles help, but self-family
preference and other biases can persist. (arXiv)
So running 365 examples through a biased/no-uncertainty selection mechanism is not necessarily more trustworthy than a
carefully designed 100-example tournament.


The tournament I would trust for Alice
Stage 0 - Public qualification
Keep exactly what we already have.

All 12 generators passed. All model digests/profiles remain frozen. The candidate/evaluator firewall remains frozen. No need to
repeat any of this.


Stage 1 - 32 common tasks × all 12 models
Keep the current Round 1 exactly.
This gives every candidate the same balanced common foundation:

  2 holdout structures
  × 4 reconstruction classes
  × 4 tasks
  = 32 tasks


That's substantially safer than my earlier proposal of 16.
And because Batch 4 is currently generating those 32 for:

  nemotron-cascade-2
  nemotron-3.5-lightning
  olmo-3.1


let it finish.
Then we change the judging/ranking system before continuing.


Stage 1 judging - redesigned
Each candidate should be judged pointwise, not alongside competing answers.
One judge sees:

  visible evidence
  hidden evaluator evidence
  ONE blinded candidate
  rubric


not six candidate outputs together.
For every candidate/task:

  4 independent judge families
  generator's own family excluded


The existing multidimensional rubric remains useful:

  evidence-conditioned fidelity
  hidden-recovery fidelity
  context/scope fidelity
  relationship fidelity
  temporal fidelity
  uncertainty calibration
  contradiction preservation
  source-person distinctiveness

  unsupported invention risk
  generic flattening risk
  gold contradiction risk


But I would change the aggregation.


Hard failures become lexicographically dominant



                                        🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                         36 / 254
Right now a sufficiently distinctive answer can mathematically compensate for some risk penalty.
I don't want that.
For Alice:

  FIRST:
  fabrication / contradiction / unsupported invention gates

  THEN:
  fidelity score

  THEN:
  distinctiveness / calibration / other secondary dimensions


A beautiful, distinctive hallucination must never outrank a slightly less distinctive evidence-grounded reconstruction.
That fits the existing Alice provenance doctrine much better.


Stage 1 elimination - no fixed top five
This is where adaptive testing actually becomes useful.
For every model we calculate a stratified bootstrap confidence interval, preserving the eight task strata.
Then maintain a credible contender set.
A model is eliminated only if there is strong evidence it is worse, for example:

  its upper confidence bound
  <
  best model's lower confidence bound - practical margin


while also checking that it isn't unusually strong on an important stratum.
So Stage 1 might produce:

  12 → 4


or:

  12 → 6


or even:

  12 → 7


if the evidence really isn't sufficient.
That is scientifically preferable to deciding ahead of time that exactly five models deserve more compute.


Stage 2 - adaptive discrimination
Before Stage 1 judging begins, we reserve a completely untouched final holdout.
From the remaining non-holdout tasks, contenders then receive new tasks in 8-task increments.
I would combine two kinds of samples:

  50% representative stratified tasks
  50% discriminative tasks


The discriminative half should be chosen from cases where the surviving models disagree most or where their strengths appear
different.




                                           🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                  37 / 254
That approach has direct support from sample-efficient evaluation work: maximum-discrepancy evaluation deliberately seeks
prompts that most distinguish candidate models, while adaptive-testing research similarly selects maximally informative items.
(ACL Anthology)
After every 8 new tasks:

  re-score
  re-bootstrap
  recompute contender probability


Models that are clearly inferior stop consuming GPU.
Maximum Stage-2 budget:

  32 extra tasks/model


But we may need only 8 or 16.
This is a proper race, not arbitrary early elimination.


Stage 3 - untouched 64-task final holdout
This is the piece my previous redesign was missing.
At tournament-v2 initialization, before seeing any new scores, freeze 64 MC8 tasks that:

    are not in the original 32;
    cannot be chosen by adaptive Stage 2;
    remain unopened to the ranking machinery;
    contain 8 tasks from each of the eight structural strata.

Finalists never get selected because they performed well on these questions.
Then the final 2-3 models generate all 64.
This gives us a genuinely independent confirmation dataset.
For the final I would increase evaluation to:

  6 independent non-generator-family judges


and keep pointwise scoring as primary.
Then add a secondary mirrored head-to-head check between #1 and #2:

  A vs B
  B vs A


on a subset.
Pairwise results don't determine the winner by themselves; they're a robustness diagnostic for ranking instability and judge
presentation bias.
That directly addresses the position/non-transitivity problems found in current LLM-evaluation research. (ACL Anthology)


Stage 4 - actual MC10B canary
This is the most important change.
The winner isn't being chosen to answer a benchmark.
It's being chosen to generate synthetic behavioral hypotheses that will influence Alice's personality-model data.
Research on synthetic-data generators specifically finds that a model's ordinary task capability does not necessarily predict its
value as a synthetic-data generator. AgoraBench reached that conclusion after producing 1.26 million synthetic examples



                                         🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                         38 / 254
and training 99 downstream models. RoSE similarly found that intrinsic generator heuristics correlate poorly with actual
downstream usefulness. (ACL Anthology)
Therefore, the top two should undergo a small real-workflow canary before we name a winner.
Something like:

  48 real MC10B gap contexts × finalist A
  48 identical contexts × finalist B


These outputs remain:

  NON-AUTHORITATIVE
  QUARANTINED
  NOT E-INF
  NOT TRAINING DATA
  NO PERSONALITY WEIGHT UPDATE
  NO MC10 CREDIT


Then run them through the actual deterministic Alice gates and measure:

    evidence conditioning;
    unsupported invention;
    appropriate UNKNOWN behavior;
    contradiction handling;
    source-person distinctiveness;
    generic flattening;
    hypothesis duplication;
    useful coverage;
    gate acceptance/quarantine/rejection profile.

That tells us how each generator behaves in the actual job we're hiring it to perform.
No model training is necessary for this canary, so we don't violate the current tournament/MC10 separation.


I would also include a small blinded owner review
This is one place where I would deliberately not trust automation alone.
A 2025 PersonaEval study found that even its best LLM evaluators achieved only about 69% role-identification accuracy, while
humans reached 90.8%. That doesn't prove our judges have 69% accuracy on Alice, but it is a serious warning against treating
LLM judgment as ground truth for subtle persona fidelity. (arXiv)
And current persona research increasingly treats fidelity as multidimensional-memory, opinions, reasoning, tone, lexical behavior,
syntax, emotion-not merely “does this sound plausible?” (ACL Anthology)
So for the top two, I would randomly select perhaps:

  24 blinded A/B pairs


No model names.
You choose:

  A
  B
  tie
  both wrong


That isn't enough to override the entire benchmark arbitrarily, but it becomes an important final validation signal.


                                       🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                         39 / 254
For a system where subtle source-person fidelity matters this much, I think excluding human-owner judgment entirely would be a
methodological mistake.


One more thing: don't force a single winner if the evidence says otherwise
Research on prompt-specific model rankings shows that different models can genuinely be best on different types of prompts.
(Proceedings of Machine Learning Research)
Synthetic-data work also increasingly finds benefits from heterogeneous generators rather than assuming one teacher dominates
universally. (ICLR Proceedings)
So v2 should be allowed to conclude either:

  PRIMARY WINNER = model X


or, if the evidence genuinely shows specialization:

  PRIMARY = model X
  SPECIALIST/CHALLENGER = model Y


I would not fuse their outputs indiscriminately because Alice's provenance needs to stay clean. But deterministic routing-e.g. Y
only for a specific class where it consistently dominates-is scientifically defensible.
If one model genuinely dominates across strata, then we use one model.


Compute impact
Once current Batch 4 finishes, the old tournament still wants roughly:

  5 × 64   = 320
  3 × 269 = 807
  ────────────────
  1,127 additional generator outputs


The new design's conservative maximum after Round 1 is approximately:

  Stage 2:
  6 contenders × max 32 = 192

  Final:
  3 × 64 = 192

  Real MC10B canary:
  2 × 48 = 96
  ──────────────────
  480 maximum


And because Stage 2 evaluates in increments of 8, a realistic run could be closer to 350-400.
So we're still cutting roughly 60-70% of the remaining generation work, but without making the risky assumption that 16
benchmark cases are enough to choose Alice's synthetic-data generator.
That's a much better trade.
Kaggle itself currently documents 12-hour GPU notebook limits and finite weekly GPU quotas, so avoiding exhaustive unnecessary
generation also matters operationally. (Kaggle)

What you should do with the tournament right now
Do not Ctrl+C yet.
Let:

  round1-generation-p1-b4



                                       🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                          40 / 254
finish.
The moment your tracker changes to something like:

  request_key=round1-judging-p1-b1


that means the last three models' Round-1 outputs have been downloaded/accepted and the old controller is beginning its
obsolete judge phase.
Then Ctrl+C the actual tournament terminal.
If a judging kernel already launches before you catch it, that's fine-we can delete that judge kernel. We won't use partial v1
judging in the redesigned tournament because the judge protocol itself is changing.
So my final verdict is:

  Yes, redesign the tournament-but not into the earlier 16/8/16 version.
  Finish the current 32-task screen for all 12, then switch to a confidence-gated adaptive tournament + untouched
  final holdout + real MC10B canary + small blinded owner validation.

Given how consequential this generator is for Alice, this is the first design in this sequence that I would be comfortable using to
make the actual generator-selection decision.

Before this failure it successfully:

1. froze the exact valid Round-1 candidate bytes;

because you know Elaina deeply, but that can also introduce expectation bias and preference for memorable stylistic cues.

  Which is more evidence-conditioned?

  Overall fidelity preference?

  Set-Location C:\A.L.I.C.E-main

  $Zip = "$HOME\Downloads\ALICE_Kaggle_Tournament_ADAPTIVE_V2_v2.0.0.zip"
  $Expected = "740A76692C00CC665D615EC0B7AF3FB52123DF3A382CD3CF54D8FA7D362A3966"

  if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Zip -PathType Leaf)) {
      throw "Missing v2 ZIP: $Zip"

  $Actual = (
      Get-FileHash `

against all three PowerShell entry scripts. Then it recompiles all Python files and reruns the v2 self-test.

  Gemma judge

are all in the same kernel. Pasted text(20260824-063535).txt

Internally, the judge only evaluates up to 8 pointwise cases per inference call, with retries and recursive splitting if structured
output fails.
So this is not Kaggle randomly hanging.
It is doing a huge serial inference workload.
That recreated exactly the head-of-line problem we removed from generator execution.

How I would fix v2
I would not go back to the exhaustive old tournament, and I would not weaken the final decision quality.
I would change only the evaluation architecture.


                                        🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                          58 / 254
Screen: 2 judges first, not 4 universally
Every one of the 364 valid candidates gets:

  Judge A
  +
  Judge B


from independent, non-generator families.
Still pointwise. Still blinded.
Then use Judges C/D only when needed:

  A and B disagree on verdict
  OR

  one detects fabrication/contradiction
  and the other does not
  OR

  risk scores disagree materially
  OR

  quality judgments are far apart
  OR

  the model is close to the
  elimination confidence boundary


So instead of:

  364 × 4 = 1,456 mandatory judgments


baseline becomes:

  364 × 2 = 728


plus targeted adjudication.
This is much better than simply cutting to two judges permanently, because ambiguous/high-risk cases still receive four-family
review.

Make screen judging compact
The current screen asks every judge for 11 numeric dimensions + verdict + explanation for every candidate.
That's unnecessary at the first screen.
I'd use a compact screen rubric such as:

  evidence fidelity
  hidden recovery
  context/relationship fidelity
  uncertainty calibration
  distinctiveness

  unsupported invention risk
  gold contradiction risk
  generic flattening risk

  verdict


No prose explanation unless the case is referred to adjudication.


                                          🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                      59 / 254
Full 11-dimensional evaluation stays for finalists.
That means more candidate cases fit safely into each inference call and substantially less output has to be generated.


One judge model per Kaggle kernel
This becomes a hard rule:

  Judge kernel 1 → Qwen only
  Judge kernel 2 → Gemma only
  Judge kernel 3 → Muse only
  ...


Never again:

  one kernel
    Qwen
    ↓
    Gemma
    ↓
    Muse


If Qwen is slow, we know Qwen is slow.
If Gemma fails, Qwen's completed work doesn't disappear.
And if Kaggle permits concurrent commit capacity at that moment, separate jobs can potentially benefit from it without making
concurrency a correctness requirement.


Later stages become stricter, not weaker
The hierarchy becomes:

  SCREEN
  32 tasks
  2 judges
  + targeted 3rd/4th adjudication

          ↓

  ADAPTIVE CONTENDERS
  4 judges

          ↓

  64-TASK FINAL HOLDOUT
  6 judges

          ↓

  mirrored A/B diagnostic

          ↓

  blinded owner validation

          ↓

          ↓




                                         🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                     60 / 254
  WINNER


So we spend fewer judgments on obviously weak models and more independent scrutiny as a model gets closer to
becoming Alice's generator.
That's the allocation I actually want.

One research point matters here
I considered switching screen judging back to multi-candidate/listwise prompts because it would be dramatically faster.
I'm not recommending that as the primary fix.
Current research confirms that both listwise and pairwise LLM judging exhibit real position bias, and that it varies across judges
and tasks. (ACL Anthology)
So for something as consequential as Alice's synthetic personality hypotheses, I'd rather retain pointwise independence and
reduce cost through progressive adjudication, compact rubrics, and better execution scheduling.
That is a safer compromise than shoving all 12 candidates into one giant comparison prompt.

One more thing I noticed
V2 imported:

  384 total frozen candidate records


but only:

  364 valid pointwise candidates


entered judging. Build-ALICEStageG2APriority4AElainaAdjudicationAndEIPMLanes-v1.0.0.ps1
That means 20 of the original generator results are invalid/non-judgeable records.
That isn't necessarily wrong-the v2 core deliberately preserves missing/failed generation as invalid candidates rather than
fabricating replacements-but before the next run I want those 20 explicitly audited by model/task. A model should not
accidentally benefit or suffer because its failed outputs were handled incorrectly in the ranking.
So yes: stop p1b1 now.
The scientifically important parts of Tournament v2 stay. The judging execution layer needs one more redesign before we burn
any more Kaggle GPU time.

where more evidence is needed. Any model that would be dropped gets a paired 4-judge adjudication subset against the current

generator.

Several additional fixes came out of the audit.
The primary judge panel is no longer arbitrarily hard-coded. V2.1 reads the frozen v1 public qualification timing receipts
and deterministically chooses the four fastest already-qualified judge families. Their receipt hashes become part of the protocol
lock. Speed only affects which judges do the cheap, non-eliminating primary screen; all qualified judge families remain eligible for
adjudication and the final.
Each primary candidate gets exactly two independent non-generator-family judges. A two-judge result cannot eliminate a
generator. Any model provisionally headed for elimination receives at least 16 paired protection tasks against the primary
reference and both sides are raised to four independent judges before elimination is even possible.
Compact screen requests are limited to 12 isolated candidates / roughly 42K serialized characters, 2,048 output tokens, and a 20-
minute per-inference timeout instead of the old 30-minute ceiling.
The 20 invalid/non-judgeable v1 candidate records are now explicitly audited before judging. They are not silently dropped and
are not mislabeled as fabrication. They get utility 0 and contribute to a separate invalid-generation rate; invalid-rate elimination
still requires the 99% confidence gate.


                                        🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                            64 / 254
I also fixed the cross-stage rubric issue: compact screen raw scores are never mixed mathematically with the full 11-
dimensional adaptive rubric. Adaptive scoring reuses the frozen screen task utility and combines it only with full-rubric scores
from newly generated adaptive tasks, with screen/adaptive bootstrap strata kept separate.
Downloaded judge outputs are now independently validated for exact assignment coverage, identity/digest/profile, score field set,
numeric ranges, verdict enum, provenance flags, and rubric type. Adaptive/final generator outputs are likewise independently
revalidated against their exact visible-evidence sets. A cached proposal that cites an evidence ID the generator wasn't allowed to
see is rejected.
Remote judge identities are now short but cryptographically bound to:

  stage
  + judge model
  + exact judge-input SHA256


so Kaggle's 45-character slug limit cannot truncate away the unique identity and create reconnect collisions.
And owner review becomes immutable after successful submission: a conflicting second submission fails closed rather than
silently replacing the first.

& {
      $ErrorActionPreference = "Stop"

      Set-Location C:\A.L.I.C.E-main

      $Zip = "$HOME\Downloads\ALICE_Kaggle_Tournament_ADAPTIVE_V2_1_v2.1.1.zip"
      $Expected = "ACE5FED91A871AEE15C392C83C5D6C3E2831359F65CCB892DDEA5AEEA083F01F"
      $Bundle = "$HOME\Downloads\ALICE_Kaggle_Tournament_ADAPTIVE_V2_1_v2.1.1"

      Write-Host "===== V2.1.1 PACKAGE CHECK =====" -ForegroundColor Cyan

      if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Zip -PathType Leaf)) {
          throw "Missing v2.1.1 ZIP: $Zip"
      }

      $Actual = (
          Get-FileHash `
              -LiteralPath $Zip `
              -Algorithm SHA256
      ).Hash.ToUpperInvariant()

      Write-Host "ZIP_SHA256=$Actual"

      if ($Actual -ne $Expected) {
          throw "v2.1.1 ZIP hash mismatch. Expected=$Expected Actual=$Actual"
      }

      if (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Bundle) {
          Write-Host "Existing v2.1.1 extraction found: $Bundle"
      }
      else {
          Expand-Archive `
              -LiteralPath $Zip `
              -DestinationPath "$HOME\Downloads"
      }

      $Starter = Join-Path `
          $Bundle `
          "Start-ALICEAdaptiveTournamentV2_1-v2.1.1.ps1"

      if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Starter -PathType Leaf)) {
          throw "v2.1.1 starter missing: $Starter"
      }

      Unblock-File -LiteralPath $Starter -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue

      Write-Host ""
      Write-Host "===== RESUMING V2.1 WORKROOT IN QUOTA MODE =====" -ForegroundColor Cyan
      Write-Host "Target checkpoint: completed + scored primary screen"
      Write-Host "No adjudication GPU stage may launch."

      & powershell.exe `
          -NoProfile `
          -ExecutionPolicy Bypass `
          -File $Starter `
          -RepoRoot "C:\A.L.I.C.E-main" `
          -VaultRoot "C:\ALICE_Vault" `
          -ReconnectMinutes 45 `
          -QuotaStopAfterScreenPrimary

      $Code = $LASTEXITCODE

  & {
        $ErrorActionPreference = "Stop"

        Set-Location C:\A.L.I.C.E-main

        $Zip = "$HOME\Downloads\ALICE_Kaggle_Tournament_ADAPTIVE_V2_1_v2.1.2.zip"
        $Expected = "72423BC407825F9139BCBCDF863845F80E9C25ED8A74380DA13599CA15DAFD76"
        $Bundle = "$HOME\Downloads\ALICE_Kaggle_Tournament_ADAPTIVE_V2_1_v2.1.2"

        Write-Host "===== V2.1.2 RECOVERY PACKAGE CHECK =====" -ForegroundColor Cyan

        if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Zip -PathType Leaf)) {
            throw "Missing v2.1.2 ZIP: $Zip"
        }

        $Actual = (
            Get-FileHash `
                -LiteralPath $Zip `
                -Algorithm SHA256
        ).Hash.ToUpperInvariant()

        Write-Host "ZIP_SHA256=$Actual"

        if ($Actual -ne $Expected) {
            throw "v2.1.2 ZIP hash mismatch. Expected=$Expected Actual=$Actual"

if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Bundle)) {
    Expand-Archive `
        -LiteralPath $Zip `

    $Bundle `
    "Start-ALICEAdaptiveTournamentV2_1-v2.1.2.ps1"

if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Starter -PathType Leaf)) {
    throw "v2.1.2 starter missing: $Starter"

There are actually two execution bugs involved:

1. Kaggle has a recently reported failure mode where a kernel slug rejected around session/concurrency handling can become
   effectively “bricked”: repeated pushes of the exact same slug continue returning 404/Notebook-not-found, while using a new
   slug succeeds. That matches GLM/Muse very closely. (kaggle.com)
2. Our v2.1 helper says judge_job_max_attempts=2 , but attempt 2 was using the same stable job identity as attempt 1. So
   Qwen/Gemma's rejected outputs would simply be rediscovered instead of receiving a genuine second run.

I fixed both in v2.1.5.
Download v2.1.5
SHA-256:

  7E379906948432347483922755CB55D4AAA859897BCED15A1BFC918CB735DC5B



What v2.1.5 does
The methodology is unchanged. There are still at most 2 judge attempts. The scoring core, tournament protocol, stage worker,
and judge worker remain unchanged.
Execution now works as originally intended:

  ATTEMPT 1
      │
      ├─ valid output → accept
      │
      ├─ completed but invalid → preserve
      │                           ↓
      │                    ATTEMPT 2
      │                    NEW deterministic identity
      │
      └─ repeated 404 on same slug
             ↓
         max 6 same-slug repushes
             ↓
         authenticated owner-list confirms absent
             ↓
         mark old slug suspected-bricked
             ↓
         ATTEMPT 2
         NEW deterministic identity


Crucially, attempt 1 identities are backward-compatible. So the existing Stage 1B work isn't thrown away.
If GLM or Muse actually materialized remotely just before you hit Ctrl+C , v2.1.5 sees that and reconnects instead of launching a
duplicate.

I’m patching only the Stage-1B execution controller, not the tournament protocol. The target behavior is: a settled rc=78
immediately consumes the authorized retry path in the same controller run; only a predecessor that is genuinely still RUNNING
may cause a safe 75 pause.
I found a second problem in the same loop that I’m fixing at the same time because it affects safety: v2.1.7 submits all nine
judge jobs into the executor up front. So even after one job reports a genuine 75 running predecessor, queued jobs can
continue dispatching before the controller finally pauses. v2.1.8 will use a bounded scheduler: never more than two live dispatch
slots, and once a genuine running predecessor is discovered it stops feeding new work.
The retry state machine is now clear: model-attempt count and infrastructure-identity count must be separate. A
completed-but-invalid judge consumes attempt 1 and moves to attempt 2; a 404/absent kernel gets a new execution identity
while staying on the same model attempt. That prevents Kaggle infrastructure failures from silently exhausting the two allowed
judge attempts.

      I’m interpreting the goal as retrying settled 78 jobs immediately during the same run, while preserving pauses only for predecessors
      that are genuinely still running. I’m checking how concurrency limits affect whether those retries can proceed safely.

  ●

      a bounded scheduler that halts new launches after detecting active predecessors while retrying eligible settled jobs immediately.

Done. v2.1.8 is ready. This is the execution-only fix we wanted; the tournament methodology is unchanged.
Download ALICE_Kaggle_Tournament_ADAPTIVE_V2_1_v2.1.8.zip
SHA-256:

  5A14ADA0EB62CACC9068F8CBCDA04BB3A36D4D9B6BDE9CC023212C8E0F5527C1



What v2.1.8 fixes
The retry system now separates:

  MODEL ATTEMPT
      ≠
  KAGGLE EXECUTION IDENTITY


So:

  completed/model-failure/invalid attempt 1
          ↓
  model attempt 1 consumed
          ↓
  fresh ATTEMPT 2 immediately
          ↓
  same controller run


But:

  404 / kernel proven never materialized
          ↓
  model attempt NOT consumed
          ↓
  fresh execution identity r1
          ↓
  still MODEL ATTEMPT 1


If that fresh infrastructure identity also never materializes, v2.1.8 fails closed without touching attempt 2.
I also fixed the concurrency bug. v2.1.7 queued all nine judge jobs before learning that old kernels were still running. v2.1.8 now
uses a lazy bounded scheduler:

  2 remote slots total

  0 blockers → dispatch up to 2
  1 running predecessor → use only 1 remaining slot
  2 running predecessors → dispatch NOTHING else → safe 75


So queued jobs can no longer spill onto Kaggle after the two remote slots are already occupied.


Tests passed
The clean ZIP re-extraction passed:

  clean_extract_manifest_verified=true
  clean_extract_exact_file_set_verified=true
  clean_extract_python_compile_passed=true
  clean_extract_all_selftests_passed=true
  methodology_and_dispatcher_critical_bytes_unchanged=true
  controller_change_only_version_banner=true


And the new real-state tests passed:

 verified model_failure advances to attempt 2    ✅

What v2.1.9 changes
Before any new Stage-1B Kaggle launch:

  scan preserved Stage-1B dispatcher states
          ↓
  probe exact owned Kaggle kernel refs
          ↓
  identify currently RUNNING predecessors
          ↓
  reserve their GPU slots FIRST


Then:

  2 running predecessors
  → 2/2 slots reserved
  → ZERO new launches
  → exit 75 safely

  1 running predecessor
  → 1/2 slots reserved
  → only ONE new dispatch allowed at a time

  0 running predecessors
  → normal max-2 scheduling

  indeterminate Kaggle state
  → ZERO new launches
  → exit 75 fail-closed


Mapped running jobs are also removed from that run's dispatch queue entirely, so we don't waste time asking the ordinary
dispatcher to rediscover something preflight already proved is running.
The v2.1.8 distinction remains unchanged:

  Kaggle infrastructure identity failure
          ≠
  model inference attempt


So Qwen/Gemma's failed-to-materialize attempt-2 identities still do not consume their legitimate second model inference
attempt.

📘 Executive Summary

    Executive Summary
    Local alternatives to Kaggle tournaments can dramatically speed up ALICE’s model-selection loop by evaluating
    candidate LLMs on-premise. Modern open-source models (e.g. Meta’s LLaMA family, Mistral AI, EleutherAI’s GPT-OSS
    models) can run on CPUs (via quantization/llama.cpp) or a single GPU. We propose three tiers of workflow – Fast
    (minimal setup), Medium (small benchmark), and Thorough (comprehensive testing) – each outlining steps, time
    estimates, and costs for CPU vs. GPU. Key evaluation metrics include fluency, coherence, relevance, and factuality,
    plus specialized “memory-utility” measures aligned to ALICE’s needs. We recommend a lightweight benchmark of ~20–
    50 memory-oriented prompts, a rubric scoring each output for the above metrics, and scripts (bash/Python) to automate
    it. We provide example prompts and pseudocode for running models locally (e.g. via llama.cpp or Hugging Face
    pipelines). A decision table compares model families (LLaMA-2/3, Mistral, GPT-J/NeoX, etc.) on parameters like latency,
    memory usage, and expected output quality. For synthetic memory generation, we discuss few-shot templates, LLM
    distillation, and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), with validation protocols. Finally, we highlight risks (e.g.
    hallucination, compute limits) and telemetry suggestions (logging key metrics in ALICE’s Git/JSON telemetry). Integrating
    results into ALICE’s pipeline ensures reproducibility and auditability.

    1. Available Local Models and Hardware
    Modern open-source LLMs enable local inference even without GPUs. For example, Llama (Meta) models (2 and 3
    series) are available from 7B to 70B parameters under permissive licenses; the smaller 7–8B variants perform
    comparably to much larger models. Llama 3 uses Group-Query Attention (GQA) for efficiency. Mistral AI provides 7B and
    mixture-of-experts models (e.g. “Mixtral”) with open licenses. EleutherAI’s GPT-OSS family (e.g. GPT-J 6B, GPT-NeoX 20B)
    and Hugging Face’s BLOOM (~7B) also run locally. Many of these have lightweight instruction-tuned versions.
    On CPU-only hardware, inference is slow but feasible with quantization. Projects like llama.cpp (a C/C++ framework)
    allow running quantized models on CPU and GPU. For instance, a quantized Mistral-7B GGUF model ran on an old
    MacBook Pro (no GPU) using llama.cpp. Llama.cpp’s design supports CUDA, Metal (Apple M1/M2), and even OpenCL/CPU
    accelerators. In practice, quantized Llama-8B models can run in ~10 GB RAM (freeing ~50 GB after quantization) using
    Intel’s PyTorch extensions. Therefore, on a modern multi-core CPU (especially with AVX-512 or Apple M chip), even 7–8B
    models are usable, albeit slower than GPU. On a single mid-range GPU (e.g. RTX 3080 or A100), these models run in
    seconds per prompt.
    Hardware scenarios: With CPU-only, expect low throughput (e.g. a few tokens/sec); use quantization (INT4/INT8) and
    optimized libs (Intel iPEX). With one GPU, moderate-sized models (up to ~13B on 24GB GPU) are practical with FP16 or
    int8. For larger models (e.g. Llama2-70B), a single 40GB GPU (A100) might fit 7B at a time.

    2. Concrete Workflow Scenarios

                                       🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                         158 / 254
Fast Workflow (minimal, ~1–3 hours): Use a CPU or single GPU with no fine-tuning.

   Step 1: Install inference framework (e.g. pip install llama-cpp-python or Hugging Face Transformers).
   Step 2: Select 1–2 candidate models (e.g. Llama-2-7B, Mistral-7B). Download/preload them.
   Step 3: Prepare a tiny prompt set (e.g. 5–10 prompts from ALICE use-cases). For CPU, use llama.cpp CLI or Hugging
   Face pipeline; on GPU, use Transformers.
   Step 4: Run inference, log outputs. For CPU, this may take minutes per prompt (e.g. ~50 tokens in ~30–60 s); on
   GPU, seconds or less.
   Step 5: Quickly eyeball or auto-score (see Sec. 3 below) for fluency/coherence/relevance. Choose the best model(s).
   Time Estimate: ~1–2 hours wall-clock (CPU) or <30 min (GPU). (~2–4 CPU-core-hours vs. ~0.1 GPU-hour.)

Medium Workflow (moderate, ~1 day): Build on Fast by adding more models/prompts.

   Steps: As above, but include ~3–5 models (e.g. add GPT-J, Llama-2-13B) and ~20–30 prompts (covering key
   memory-generation tasks). Optionally run small “few-shot” loops (add 1–5 examples in prompts).
   Compute: On CPU (4–8 cores), expect several hours for ~1000 total tokens per model (so maybe 3–6 h per model).
   On GPU, likely <1 h per model.
   Outcome: Automated scores for each model on each metric; identify top 1–2 models. Possibly fine-tune them with
   example memory snippets (Sec. 4).
   Time Estimate: ~0.5–1 day (CPU); ~1–2 hours (GPU).

Thorough Workflow (comprehensive, multi-day): Full comparative benchmark and tuning.

   Steps: Gather 5–10 candidate models. Define a held-out “validation” prompt set (~50 prompts) and a development
   set (~50 prompts). Run all models on the dev set to collect metrics (may use batch scripts or prompt engineering
   frameworks). Optionally apply light fine-tuning (e.g. LoRA with a few memory examples) on the top 2–3 models.
   Finally, evaluate tuned and untuned models on the held-out set.
   Compute: CPU-only could take a full day or more (e.g. 10 models × 1000 tokens = 10k tokens; at ~5 tok/s, ~2000 s
   ≈ 33 min per model, ~6 h total). GPU can do it in under 1 h. Few-shot tuning (LoRA) might add a few hours on GPU
   (versus infeasible on CPU).
   Outcome: Detailed comparison table of model performance vs. cost. Select final model(s).
   Time Estimate: ~1–2 days (CPU, including tuning); ~2–4 hours (GPU).


 timeline
     title Model-Evaluation Workflow Comparison
     section Fast Workflow
       Day 0: Install tools; pick 1–2 models
       Day 0: Run quick prompts on CPU/GPU
       Day 1: Quick scoring (manual/auto); select model
       Day 1: Record best model (telemetry update)
     section Thorough Workflow
       Day 0–1: Install and download all candidate models
       Day 1: Prepare benchmark prompts (dev/test sets)
       Day 1–2: Run dev-set inference on all models (CPU/GPU)
       Day 2: Score outputs (scripted rubric)
       Day 2: Few-shot train top models (LoRA on GPU)
       Day 3: Evaluate on held-out prompts
       Day 3: Analyze results; update ALICE telemetry/Git


Figure: Timeline of a Fast vs. Thorough model-evaluation workflow.

3. Benchmark Suite and Evaluation
Prompts & Dataset: We recommend ~20–50 domain-relevant prompts that reflect ALICE’s synthetic-memory use
cases. These could be chat or instruction prompts like “Summarize this personal anecdote for memory storage” or “Given


                                  🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                      159 / 254
context X, generate a concise memory note”. Ensure diversity (dialogue, factual queries, personal context). Keep a small
held-out set (~20%) for final validation.
Scoring Rubric: For each output, assess:

   Relevance: Does the response address the prompt? (score 1–5)
   Coherence/Fluency: Is it grammatically smooth and logically consistent? (1–5)
   Factuality: Is any factual information correct? (1–5)
   Memory-Utility: Does the output make a plausible “memory” entry (concise, useful)? (1–5) – this is task-specific.

Automated metrics (BLEU/ROUGE/BERTScore) are optional but often mislead for open tasks. Instead, consider simple
heuristics or small-scale human review. The rubric can be implemented via a Python script. For example:

 from transformers import pipeline

 # Example Python pseudocode to run models locally and score them:
 models = {
     "llama-3-8b": pipeline("text-generation", model="meta-llama/Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct"),
     "mistral-7b": pipeline("text-generation", model="mistralai/Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3")
 }
 prompts = ["Tell me a fun fact about space.", "..."] # our benchmark prompts
 scores = {}
 for name, gen in models.items():
     outputs = gen(prompts, max_length=50)
     scores[name] = []
     for out in outputs:
         text = out['generated_text']
         # Here we would evaluate with a rubric function (or manual)
         score = evaluate_rubric(text, reference=None) # user-defined
         scores[name].append(score)
 print(scores)


Alternatively, using llama.cpp on CPU, one can batch prompts:

 # Bash pseudocode using llama.cpp CLI for Mistral 7B (quantized)
 echo "Summarize: I went to the park and had a picnic." > prompt.txt
 ./llama.cpp/main -m models/mistral-7b-instruct.Q6_K.gguf -ngl 0 -f prompt.txt -t 4 > out.txt
 # Then post-process out.txt to score it.


Sample Size: Start small to keep turns fast. E.g., 20 prompts * 5 models = 100 model runs; at ~50 tokens each, that’s
~5000 tokens. On a GPU this is trivial; on a CPU it’s measurable (tens of minutes).
Evaluation: After running, use the rubric to generate a score table (e.g. average relevance, coherence, factuality per
model). Use these to compare. Emphasize clear logging (e.g. CSV or JSON) for reproducibility. We suggest minimal human
checks to verify automated scores, especially for “factuality.”

latency/compute cost, inference hardware compatibility, and privacy/license. Table 1 summarizes key families:

Sources & Notes: LLaMA models use grouped-query attention (GQA) for efficiency. Llama.cpp benchmarks confirm it
runs on “consumer-grade” hardware. Mistral-7B (instruct) is available in quantized GGUF form and often matches or
exceeds Llama-7B performance. GPT-J/NeoX require more compute per parameter and may lag on coherence.
When selecting, prioritize models that (a) fit your hardware, (b) have strong performance on memory-like tasks, (c) allow
fast inference (e.g. int4 quantization). For example, Mistral-7B and Llama-3-8B can both be quantized to ~10GB RAM; on
GPU they produce outputs quickly with low latency. (In llama.cpp tests, Mistral-7B even outperforms Llama3-8B on high-
end GPUs, though this may reverse with optimized frameworks.)

5. Synthetic Memory Generation Methods
Creating high-quality synthetic memory entries is as crucial as model choice. Common methods include:

    Few-Shot Prompting: Provide the model with a template and examples of “memory” outputs. E.g.:

      Instruction: Summarize the following personal anecdote as a memory note.
      Example: "User says: I won a prize. Memory: User won a prize."
      Task: "User says: I had coffee with Emma."


    This steers the model to format its output as a memory. Simple few-shot examples can greatly improve consistency
    (effectively a kind of in-context learning).

    Distillation from Larger Models: Use a strong LLM (or ensemble) to generate synthetic memory entries for a
    variety of inputs, then train a smaller local model on this data. For instance, feed many ALICE prompts to GPT-4 (or a
    top open model) to get memory outputs, then fine-tune Llama-7B on that synthetic dataset. The SynAlign work shows
    LLM-synthesized data can boost smaller models, though one must align distributions carefully.


                                    🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                            161 / 254
    Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG): Incorporate external knowledge via embedding retrieval. For ALICE, one
    could store relevant facts or past conversation snippets in a vector store. At generation time, retrieve context (e.g.
    previous messages) and feed them as extra prompt. RAG grounds the output with real data, reducing hallucination.
    For memory tasks, retrieval might fetch recent user events or facts, which the model then composes into a memory
    entry. This is especially useful if the model’s base knowledge is stale.

    Memory Agent Techniques: Draw on agent-memory benchmarks. E.g. ensure the model can perform “accurate
    retrieval” by checking if a generated memory can answer future related questions. Verify “test-time learning” by
    seeing if adding one memory example influences outputs properly. These competencies guide design: your synthetic
    memory should help the model answer questions correctly and revise conflicting info.

Validation: Always validate synthetic memory quality. Possible checks:

    Spot-check Factuality: If memory entries include facts (“I met Alice on Jan 5”), verify against known info.
    Coherence Check: Ensure the memory text is well-formed.
    Utility Test: Use the memory in a downstream task. For example, prompt the LLM with the memory as context and a
    related question; see if it aids accuracy.

Example Pseudocode:

  # Pseudocode: Generate synthetic memory via RAG and few-shot prompting
  from transformers import pipeline
  generator = pipeline("text-generation", model="meta-llama/Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct")

  def generate_memory(user_text, relevant_docs):
      prompt = f"""You are an assistant that writes short memory notes.
  User said: {user_text}
  Documents: {relevant_docs}
  Memory Note:"""
      mem = generator(prompt, max_length=30)[0]['generated_text']
      return mem

  # Example usage:
  user_input = "I bought a new car today."
  docs = "On Jan 1, User was shopping for cars."
  memory = generate_memory(user_input, docs)
  print(memory) # e.g. "User bought a new car (shopping on Jan 1)."


This combines retrieved context ( docs ) with a template to produce a concise memory entry.

6. Risks, Limitations, and Telemetry
Risks: Local evaluation has limits. CPU-only tests may not reflect performance on heavier GPUs; small benchmarks may
not generalize. All LLMs risk hallucination – outputting plausible but false memories. As Singlestore notes, hallucinations
can be “convincing but entirely false” and require factual evaluation. Using RAG can mitigate this, but RAG itself
demands maintaining secure vector stores. Also, smaller models may lack nuance or long-range coherence. Mistral and
Llama perform well, but none excel at all memory-agent competencies.
Privacy & Security: Local runs keep data private, a plus. However, model biases can surface (e.g. demographics or
toxic content). Sanity-check generated memories for policy compliance.
Telemetry & Integration: To integrate with ALICE’s pipeline, record minimal but key metrics after each evaluation. For
example, output a JSON or Git-committed file containing model name, average scores for each metric, and hardware
used. ALICE telemetry could track:

    model_name , timestamp , relevance_score , coherence_score , factual_score , latency_cpu_sec , latency_gpu_sec , etc.

This could be scripted as:




                                   🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                          162 / 254
      # Example pseudocode: after evaluation
      python summarize_results.py --results metrics.json --output alice_telemetry.json
      git add alice_telemetry.json
      git commit -m "ModelX evaluation scores"


    Capture version info (model revision, code commit) to ensure reproducibility. Basic GPU usage stats (e.g. using nvidia-
    smi logs) can be included if useful.

    Compute Trade-offs: Avoid exhaustive runs if not needed. Track wall-clock and estimated cost (e.g. $ or CPU-core
    hours) in telemetry to justify choices. For instance: “Llama-2-7B on CPU took 1200s (~0.33 GPU-hrs equivalent)” – this
    helps plan future runs.
    Limitations: No local setup can fully replace large-scale evaluation or human judgement. The fast workflow may miss
    subtleties, and even thorough local tests are approximate. The external Kaggle system may have used additional
    crowdsourcing or benchmarks not replicated here. Our goal is rapid iteration: a “good enough” model under resource
    constraints. Document any unknowns (e.g., “user specifics: unspecified memory target format”), and iterate as ALICE’s
    use-case clarifies.

    References
    Key concepts drawn from LLM evaluation and inference literature: fluency/coherence metrics, factual accuracy concerns,
    and memory-agent benchmarks. Llama.cpp’s portability and quantization abilities are well-documented, and specific
    examples (Mistral on CPU, Llama 3 quantized) are shown in community tutorials. Retrieval-augmented generation
    benefits are summarized by IBM, and LLM synthetic data techniques (few-shot, distillation) are covered in recent
    research. These guide our proposed workflows and benchmarks.

The latest run reinforces that decision. It died before useful work because Kaggle authentication failed, while all scientific state

What I think we should do

1. Declare v2.1.9 superseded at screen-primary . Preserve every workroot and receipt. No more Kaggle GPU jobs. It remains
   useful forensic evidence, but it is no longer the selection mechanism.

2. Build a Cloud-Only Fast Selection Gate from the existing 384 outputs. No generator runs. No local inference. We blind
   the 12 model identities and evaluate each task as one 12-answer comparison. Instead of 712 Stage-1B assignments across
   nine local-model judges, use two strong hosted judges with different families. Each judge sees the same task and all 12
   anonymous answers in a randomized order. That is only 32 tasks × 2 judges = 64 judgment calls.
      This is much closer to established efficient evaluation practice. Arena-Hard uses automated frontier-model judges specifically
      as a cheaper/faster approximation to human preference, including multi-judge aggregation and style controls. (GitHub)
      AlpacaEval likewise demonstrates that automatic preference evaluation can achieve very high correlation with human
      rankings while running in minutes and costing under $10 for a much larger benchmark. (GitHub)

3. Use a rubric specifically for A.L.I.C.E., not generic “good writing.” I would score evidence-grounding, behavioral
   reconstruction quality, relationship/context sensitivity, useful novel inference, uncertainty calibration, distinctiveness,
   contradiction risk, invention risk, and personality flattening. Length/style get controlled so a verbose model cannot win merely
   by writing more. Each judge returns a ranked 1-12 list plus dimension scores.



                                              🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                                   165 / 254
4. Aggregate once, then stop. Use rank aggregation plus per-task wins. If one model clearly leads both judges, that becomes
   the provisional MC10B generator. If the top two are close, only those two get an extra pairwise adjudication on maybe 8-
   12 hardest existing tasks. We do not re-open all twelve models.

5. Let MC10B's existing canary be the real final test. This is the architectural simplification I think matters most. We were
   trying to make the pre-MC10 tournament prove too much. The tournament only needs to identify a strong candidate
   generator. Your already-designed MC10B canary is where the winner should prove that its hypotheses are actually useful
   enough to enter the A.L.I.C.E. reconstruction pipeline. The tournament winner should remain winner_ratified=false until that
   canary passes.

No - we should not compromise on this part of A.L.I.C.E. I agree with your priority. If the personality reconstruction is
mediocre, then even a perfect memory system, UI, agent loop, and tool stack would miss the point.
But I would change one part of what I proposed before:
I no longer think we should use the cheap cloud pass merely to pick one model and declare it “the winner.”
That would be too aggressive.
The faster design can actually be at least as fidelity-preserving as the tournament, because we can move the burden of
proof away from “which model wins?” and onto the thing that matters:

  Does a proposed hypothesis reconstruct Elaina correctly from held-out real Elaina evidence?

That was always the real objective. Our earlier design explicitly recognized that public benchmarks cannot tell us which model
best reconstructs a person's conditional behavior, relationships, contradictions, emotional responses, and uncertainty. ChatGPT-
Recovery Audit Progress-20260823-2255(1).pdf

What we should replace the tournament with
1. Freeze the tournament where it is
We preserve:

      all 384 Q4 generator outputs;
      all 728 completed Stage-1A judgments;
      primary task scores;
      invalid-candidate records;
      all receipts/hashes;
      every failed attempt as forensic history.

Stage 1A really did complete: 364 valid candidates received the required two primary judgments, producing 728 judgments, and
the scoring produced 11 provisional elimination models. Pasted text(20260825-073524).txt
We do not discard that work.
We simply stop spending GPUs trying to manufacture the extraordinarily high confidence required by the old Stage-1B
tournament.


2. Do not select a single generator yet
This is the biggest change.
Instead select a provisional generator set, probably 2-3 models.
Why?
Because different models can plausibly reconstruct different parts of a person better. We had already identified this risk before
the tournament: one model may be stronger at relationship/context inference while another is better calibrated about UNKNOWN ,
and another may preserve style/personality distinctions better. ChatGPT-Recovery Audit Progress-20260823-2255.pdf
Throwing all but one away prematurely can actually reduce Elaina fidelity.
So:

  12 models
     ↓
  existing 384 outputs
     +
  existing 728 judgments
     ↓
  cheap independent cloud adjudication

     ↓
  TOP 2-3 PROPOSER FAMILIES
  not "one absolute winner"



3. Cloud judging becomes an independent audit, not our truth source
This is important because LLM judges are not perfectly reliable.
Research supports automated judging as a very efficient ranking mechanism. Arena-Hard reported 98.6% correlation with
human-preference rankings for its automated benchmark at roughly $20, while AlpacaEval reports 0.98 Spearman correlation
after length control. (Proceedings of Machine Learning Research)
But automated judges have known biases. Length/verbosity effects are real, and research also finds position, style, and other
judge biases. (DOI)
Therefore we should not do:

  GPT says Qwen wins
  → Qwen becomes Alice generator


Instead:

  existing local-model judgments
              +
  2 independent frontier cloud judges
              +
  position randomization
              +
  style/length normalization
              +
  hidden model identities
              ↓
  provisional top 2-3


No judge sees model names.
No judge knows which answer came from Qwen/Gemma/Muse/etc.
Ordering gets reversed/randomized to neutralize position effects.
Length/style are controlled rather than rewarded.
And disagreement is preserved instead of averaged away.


4. Then MC10B becomes the actual fidelity contest
This is where I think we can make A.L.I.C.E. stronger than the old tournament.
Your existing MC10 doctrine already requires:

    frozen candidate-specific E0;
    provenance;
    independent discovery;
    generator/judge separation;
    blinded/challenger evaluation;
    challenge debt;
    contextual-variance preservation;
    off-target tests;
    independent final certification;
    synthetic material forbidden from certifying itself. ChatGPT-Alice Memory Continuation.pdf


                                        🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                      170 / 254
That should remain untouched.
So the top 2-3 generators enter the MC10B canary as hypothesis proposers.
For a hidden pseudo-gap:

  VISIBLE E0 ABOUT ELAINA
          │
          ├── Generator A
          ├── Generator B
          └── Generator C
                  ↓
          hypotheses frozen
                  ↓
       reveal withheld REAL E0
                  ↓
      how well did each hypothesis
         reconstruct Elaina?


That is far more meaningful than asking nine models to rate each other's prose.


5. Better still: don't permanently choose one model if the evidence says not
to
Imagine the canary discovers:

Reconstruction area                                              Best

relationship behavior                                            Model A

emotional response                                               Model B

uncertainty / abstention                                         Model C

conversational mannerisms                                        Model B

contradictions / edge cases                                      Model A


The old tournament tries to compress this into:

  WINNER = Model A


I don't think that's necessarily optimal anymore.
A.L.I.C.E. could have a hypothesis-generation router:

  gap type
     ↓
  best historically validated generator
     ↓
  primary hypothesis

  high-impact / uncertain gap?
     ↓ yes
  independent challenger generator


That gives us diversity when useful, without paying 3× generation cost everywhere.
For normal cases:

  1 generator


For high-impact identity questions:



                                       🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                 171 / 254
  primary + challenger


For ambiguous/contradictory regions:

  2-3 independent proposers


That is both cheaper and potentially more faithful.


6. We keep a hard distinction between hypothesis and memory
This is essential.
A generator output is never:

   "Elaina believed X."

It is:

   "Candidate hypothesis H says Elaina may have behaved/believed X under context C."

Then it needs promotion.
Something roughly like:

  MODEL PROPOSAL
        ↓
  E-INF candidate
        ↓
  provenance attached
        ↓
  E0 consistency
        ↓
  counterevidence search
        ↓
  contextual variance
        ↓
  challenger/falsification
        ↓
  confidence + uncertainty
        ↓
  promotion gate
        ↓
  identity completion structure


Only much later does validated synthetic reconstruction affect the learned personality representation.
That separation is already compatible with A.L.I.C.E.'s doctrine. The existing policy says synthetic data must track lineage, retain
real-world anchors, preserve diversity/rare cases, and control recursive amplification of errors. LIFELONG_LEARNING_POLICY(1).md


So are we compromising?
If we did my earlier simplistic version:
   64 cheap judgments → one winner → proceed.

A little, yes.
I wouldn't do that anymore given how important personality fidelity is.

With the revised design:


                                        🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                          172 / 254
No material compromise.
We are cutting:

    redundant GPU judge inference;
    Kaggle orchestration;
    nine-judge quorum overhead;
    repeated retries;
    expensive certainty about a single champion.

We are not cutting:

    Elaina-specific held-out testing;
    independent judgment;
    falsification;
    provenance;
    uncertainty;
    contextual variance;
    generator diversity;
    real-E0 anchors;
    canary evaluation;
    final certification.

Those are the things that actually protect A.L.I.C.E. from becoming a generic "nice assistant pretending to be Elaina."


And we're already farther along than it feels
We have done the expensive generator experiment already:
12 models × 32 real reconstruction probes = 384 outputs. ChatGPT-Recovery Audit Progress-20260823-2255(1).pdf
We also spent compute getting:
728 independent primary judgments. Pasted text(20260825-073524).txt
So we're not abandoning the experiment halfway through.
We're saying:

  That's enough screening evidence. Stop benchmarking the benchmark and start validating actual reconstruction.

I think that's the correct decision.

Yep. This does zero tournament computation. It only collects the existing frozen artifacts, preserves their relative paths, writes
a SHA-256 manifest, and creates one ZIP in Downloads.
Run this whole PowerShell block:

 & {
       $ErrorActionPreference = "Stop"

       $StageRoot = "C:\ALICE_Vault\datasets\memory_stage_g2\alice.stage-g2.g2a.gold-semantic-decomposition.v1\audits"

       $V1 = Join-Path $StageRoot `
           "alice-generator-qualification-tournament-kaggle-locked12-v1.work"

       $V21 = Join-Path $StageRoot `
           "alice-generator-qualification-tournament-adaptive-v2.1.work"

       if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $V1 -PathType Container)) {
           throw "Missing v1 workroot: $V1"
       }

       if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $V21 -PathType Container)) {
           throw "Missing v2.1 workroot: $V21"
       }

       $Stamp = Get-Date -Format "yyyyMMdd-HHmmss"

       $Bundle = Join-Path $HOME `
           "Downloads\ALICE_CLOUD_SELECTOR_INPUT_$Stamp"

       $Zip = "$Bundle.zip"

       New-Item `
           -ItemType Directory `
           -Path $Bundle `
           -Force |
           Out-Null

       Write-Host "`n===== A.L.I.C.E. CLOUD SELECTOR ARTIFACT COLLECTION =====" `
           -ForegroundColor Cyan

       Write-Host "V1:   $V1"
       Write-Host "V2.1: $V21"
       Write-Host "OUT: $Bundle"

       # ------------------------------------------------------------
       # Collect relevant immutable/frozen tournament evidence only.


                                         🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                          175 / 254
# No model inference, judging, scoring, or tournament execution.
# ------------------------------------------------------------

$AllowedExtensions = @(
    ".json",
    ".jsonl",
    ".csv",
    ".txt"
)

$RelevantNameRegex = @(
    "candidate",
    "screen",
    "task",
    "strata",
    "partition",
    "evaluator",
    "evaluation",
    "gold",
    "hidden",
    "judgment",
    "judge",
    "score",
    "ranking",
    "protocol",
    "freeze",
    "blind",
    "assignment",
    "invalid",
    "contender",
    "elimination",
    "checkpoint",
    "manifest",
    "receipt"
) -join "|"

$Roots = @(
    @{
        Name = "v1"
        Path = $V1
    },
    @{
        Name = "v2.1"
        Path = $V21
    }
)

$Collected = @()

foreach ($Root in $Roots) {

    Write-Host "`nScanning $($Root.Name)..." -ForegroundColor Yellow

    $Files = Get-ChildItem `
        -LiteralPath $Root.Path `
        -File `
        -Recurse `
        -ErrorAction Stop |
        Where-Object {
            ($AllowedExtensions -contains $_.Extension.ToLowerInvariant()) -and
            (
                $_.Name -match $RelevantNameRegex -or



                               🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                      176 / 254
                $_.DirectoryName -match $RelevantNameRegex
            )
        }

    foreach ($File in $Files) {

        $Relative = $File.FullName.Substring(
            $Root.Path.Length
        ).TrimStart("\","/")

        $Destination = Join-Path `
            (Join-Path $Bundle $Root.Name) `
            $Relative

        $DestinationDir = Split-Path `
            -Parent `
            $Destination

        New-Item `
            -ItemType Directory `
            -Path $DestinationDir `
            -Force |
            Out-Null

        Copy-Item `
            -LiteralPath $File.FullName `
            -Destination $Destination `
            -Force

        $Collected += [pscustomobject]@{
            SourceRoot   = $Root.Name
            RelativePath = $Relative
            Bytes        = $File.Length
            SHA256       = (
                Get-FileHash `
                    -LiteralPath $File.FullName `
                    -Algorithm SHA256
            ).Hash.ToUpperInvariant()
        }
    }
}

if ($Collected.Count -eq 0) {
    throw "No tournament artifacts were collected."
}

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Write machine-readable inventory.
# ------------------------------------------------------------

$InventoryJson = Join-Path `
    $Bundle `
    "ARTIFACT_INVENTORY.json"

$Collected |
    Sort-Object SourceRoot, RelativePath |
    ConvertTo-Json -Depth 6 |
    Set-Content `
        -LiteralPath $InventoryJson `
        -Encoding UTF8

$Manifest = Join-Path `



                                  🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter   177 / 254
           $Bundle `
           "SHA256SUMS.txt"

      $ManifestLines = foreach (
          $Item in (
              $Collected |
              Sort-Object SourceRoot, RelativePath
          )
      ) {
          "$($Item.SHA256) $($Item.SourceRoot)/$($Item.RelativePath.Replace('\','/'))"
      }

      $ManifestLines |
          Set-Content `
              -LiteralPath $Manifest `
              -Encoding ASCII

      # ------------------------------------------------------------
      # Explicitly record the source workroots and purpose.
      # ------------------------------------------------------------

      $Readme = Join-Path `
          $Bundle `
          "README_FOR_CHATGPT.txt"

    @"
A.L.I.C.E. CLOUD-ONLY GENERATOR SELECTION INPUT

Purpose:
Recover and analyze already-generated tournament evidence without
performing any additional local or Kaggle model inference.

Sources:

V1:
$V1

V2.1:
$V21

Intended use:
- recover the frozen 12 x 32 = 384 Round-1 generator outputs
- recover frozen task/evaluator material where present
- recover existing Stage-1A judgments
- recover Stage-1A scores and elimination evidence
- recover protocol / partition / freeze / provenance receipts
- perform subsequent generator-selection analysis cloud-side

This bundle itself performs no tournament computation.
"@ |
        Set-Content `
            -LiteralPath $Readme `
            -Encoding UTF8

      Write-Host "`n===== COLLECTION SUMMARY =====" `
          -ForegroundColor Cyan

      Write-Host "Collected files: $($Collected.Count)"

      $TotalBytes = (
          $Collected |
          Measure-Object `



                                     🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                       178 / 254
        -Property Bytes `
        -Sum
).Sum

Write-Host (
    "Collected size: {0:N2} MB" -f (
        $TotalBytes / 1MB
    )
)

# Important sanity checks.
$CandidateFiles = @(
    $Collected |
    Where-Object {
        $_.RelativePath -match "candidates?\.jsonl$"
    }
)

$JudgmentFiles = @(
    $Collected |
    Where-Object {
        $_.RelativePath -match "judg"
    }
)

$ScoreFiles = @(
    $Collected |
    Where-Object {
        $_.RelativePath -match "score"
    }
)

Write-Host "Candidate JSONL files: $($CandidateFiles.Count)"
Write-Host "Judgment-related files: $($JudgmentFiles.Count)"
Write-Host "Score-related files: $($ScoreFiles.Count)"

if ($CandidateFiles.Count -eq 0) {
    throw "No candidates.jsonl files found. Do NOT upload an incomplete bundle."
}

if ($JudgmentFiles.Count -eq 0) {
    throw "No judgment artifacts found. Do NOT upload an incomplete bundle."
}

if ($ScoreFiles.Count -eq 0) {
    throw "No score artifacts found. Do NOT upload an incomplete bundle."
}

# ------------------------------------------------------------

    throw "ZIP already exists unexpectedly: $Zip"

    -CompressionLevel Optimal
